Platts Asia and Middle Eastern Gasoil Daily Market Analysis
Taiwan's Formosa sells 300,000 barrels of gasoil for Nov 6-10 loading
Thailand's SPRC offers 33,000 mt of gasoil for Oct 2628 loading
The Asian ultra-low sulfur gasoil market continued to weaken on Oct. 7 amid a longer supply outlook with Russia mulling a partial easing of its diesel export ban.
Brokers pegged the front-month November-December Singapore gasoil swaps time spread at $4.10/barrel in midafternoon Asian trading Oct. 7, narrowing for the fourth consecutive session from $4.35/b at the Oct. 6 Asian close at 0830 GMT.
On physical cargoes, the benchmark Platts-assessed cash differential for FOB Singapore gasoil with a maximum sulfur content of 10 parts per million plummeted $1.06/b day over day to $2.09/b at the Oct. 6 Asian close at 0830 GMT.
Russia could partially relax its diesel export ban by allowing some producers to export the product, Platts earlier reported.
This measure could be implemented in October to prevent refineries from overstocking, the report said.
Russia is in the process of switching to winter-grade diesel,

with demand for summer grades winding down, according to market sources. Any summer diesel still being produced will take up storage space, so lifting the ban would be preferable.
Lengthening the supply outlook, Saudi Arabia's energy minister Prince Abdulaziz bin Salman said on Oct. 6 that the EastWest pipeline is flowing at 5.8 million b/d out of its 7 million b/d capacity, with the pipeline having returned to operations within "five or six days" after it shut following Sept. 10 drone attacks.
In tenders, Taiwan's Formosa has sold 300,000 barrels of 500 ppm sulfur gasoil loading over Nov. 6-10 from Mailiao, to an Asia-based trading house at a discount of around $8.50/b to the November average of the Mean of Platts Singapore 10 ppm sulfur gasoil assessments, FOB.
Meanwhile, Thailand's SPRC issued a sell tender offering 33,000 mt of 50 ppm sulfur gasoil loading over Oct. 26-28. The tender closes on Oct. 7, with same-day validity.
According to Asia-based traders, SPRC had previously sold two similarly-sized cargoes loading over Oct. 20-24 and Oct. 24-28 at a discount of around $3.50/b and $4.50/b to Mean of Platts Singapore 10 ppm sulfur gasoil assessments, FOB.
Thai refiners were expected to supply more 50 ppm sulfur gasoil cargoes following the government's continued suspension of crude and petroleum product exports since March 1, as well as lower domestic demand stemming from the monsoon season.
"Thai refiners have all maxed runs due to good margins, and would normally export 10 ppm sulfur gasoil, but the government mandate still does not allow them to do so; they are exporting off-spec barrels -- they are long gasoil during the rainy season, and the flooding has caused domestic demand to be lower as well," a Singapore-based trader said.
The Thailand Meteorological Department warned on Oct. 5 of further risk of rain and flash flooding as the death toll from floods across the country since Sept. 16 rose to 31, according to local new media reports.
In statistical news, India's middle distillate demand rose 1.8% month over month and 5% year over year to 7.93 million mt in September, provisional data from the Petroleum Planning and Analysis Cell showed Oct. 7.
India consumed 7.16 million mt (1.78 million barrels/day) of gasoil in September, up 2% from the 11-month low of 7.02 million mt in August, the data showed. Gasoil demand increased 5.4% from 6.79 million mt in September 2025, according to the data.
The country's cumulative gasoil demand over JanuarySeptember rose 5% year over year to 72.32 million mt, the data showed.
-- Mei huey Ng

Platts assessed the front-month ICE low sulfur futures contract up $90.50/mt to $1,421/mt on the day, amid renewed attacks on vessel transits in the Strait of Hormuz.
Ship traffic through the strait dropped to 19 transits Oct. 6, a second consecutive decline and down from 23, as ship attacks continued in the narrow waterway, according to the latest report from S&P Global Commodities at Sea.
A fire broke out on board the Panama-flagged On Peace crude oil tanker as it was struck in the strait Oct. 6, nine nautical miles off the coast of Oman, maritime security group Vanguard Tech reported.
Gasoil futures may also have risen as G7 oil stock releases have been slow to materialize.
The details of the G7 members' release of up to 100 million barrels of oil are not due to be finalized until an International Energy Agency meeting Oct. 14-15, according to media reports.
"This is hardly the rapid release Washington had hoped for. With the details not being finalized until mid-October, relatively little diesel may reach the market before the US midterm elections. If diesel prices remain high, we believe the risk increases that the market starts pricing in a US diesel export ban again," said Arne Lohmann Rasmussen, Chief Analyst at Global Risk Management, in a research note Oct. 7
Moreover, some market participants are skeptical of any real improvement to supply following the stock release.
"In the mid- to long term, it could create some difficulties on the core physical reserves as these countries would need to buy product to refill those reserves," said a Mediterranean diesel trader on the day.
Additionally, the recent decline in European diesel flat prices had weakened domestic refining margins, which could exacerbate tightness.
The physical ARA diesel crack stood at $53.82/b on Oct. 6, compared with $70.24/b for jet, implying a regrade of $16.43/b in favor of jet production, Platts data showed.
"If refineries are not willing to refine diesel anymore as margins keep dropping ... there will be some lack of product," said the Mediterranean diesel trader.
Platts is part of S&P Global Energy. -- Sophia Aung
Marine Fuel
Mediterranean bunker Hi-5 spreads hit multimonth lows on tight HSFO
Bunker demand subdued across the Mediterranean
VLSFO availability remains well offered
Bunker Hi-5 spreads - the difference between very low sulfur and high sulfur fuel oil - across the Mediterranean fell to multimonth lows Oct. 7 as HSFO prices firmed, while VLSFO prices softened.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed Hi-5 spreads at

six-month lows Oct. 7 of $20/metric ton in Gibraltar, $15/mt in Algeciras, $20/mt in Las Palmas and $126/mt in Piraeus, while Istanbul's spread reached a nine-month low of $39/mt.
HSFO availability was constrained, with limited supply across the Mediterranean market. "There were tight availabilities on HSFO in Europe [...] not many cargoes around," a supplier said.
"We think all suppliers are short [of HSFO]" in Gibraltar, the supplier added.
This shortage was also evident in nearby Algeciras. "We are supplying contracts only," another supplier said, adding "[we are] not offering [HSFO] in [the] spot market." However, the supplier had better availability of VLSFO, with the earliest delivery date of Oct. 15. Despite rising delivered prices due to flat Piraeus prices, local refinery output underpins HSFO availability, while VLSFO and marine gasoil are driven by imports and cargoes. Thus, making prompt supply more exposed to arbitrage, blending constraints and stock levels. Bunker demand across the Mediterranean has been subdued amid declines in ICE Brent and gasoil futures. "Demand today [is] very slow as [the] market is falling," a supplier active in Istanbul and Piraeus said. Market participants noted similarly limited buying interest in Gibraltar and Algeciras. Within the bulk VLSFO markets, participants described the supply-demand complex as "well offered." Several Mediterranean refiners are producing stable volumes of 0.5% product, while closed arbitrage windows out of Europe have kept these barrels local, putting pressure on differentials. In the bulk Mediterranean HSFO market, opinions on supply were divided. One trader described the market as thin in terms of availability, adding there was "Med tightness" and there were "no barrels around," and expected those tight conditions to persist throughout October. However, a second trader said, "I think it is quite balanced the market [...] I don't see tightness." A third trader described the market as "dull" and noted that peak demand had passed in line with seasonal trends. In the paper market, Platts assessed the front-month Hi-5 spread at $56.75/mt Oct. 6, narrowing 25 cents/mt day over day.
-- Fynn Kiley, Tommy Petrou, Joseph Jaffe, Alexander Wong

Singapore's bunker fuel sales in September are estimated at 4.55 million metric tons, down 3.5% from August and 3.5% lower year over year, as weaker demand from crude and product tankers offset gains in container and dry bulk segments, according to the latest S&P Global Commodities at Sea nowcast.

Platts Asia and Middle East Fuel Oil Daily Market Analysis
LSFO market structure weakens; cash premium at over 2-month low

The decline in Singapore bunker sales came amid resilient HSFO bunker margins and prompt supply tightness in the regional fuel oil market.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed that average Singapore-delivered LSFO premiums fell 31% month over month to $61.80/mt in September from $90.10/mt in August, retreating from the elevated levels recorded in July and August, as weaker bunker demand and competitive cargo offers weighed on the market.
Singapore's HSFO market remained resilient, with market participants highlighting limited regional availability amid the absence of Russian arrivals, constrained Middle Eastern supplies, and potential demand from refiners seeking residual fuel oil feedstock alternatives apart from bunker buyers.

Platts European Fuel Oil Daily Market Analysis
VLSFO supply remains stable across Europe
Mediterranean traders split on barrel availability
European VLSFO grew heavier on Oct. 7, while participants saw mixed physical fundamentals for HSFO.
Across the European VLSFO market, availabilities remained healthy as production was stable, while arbitrage windows stayed closed.
"I see VLSFO well offered right now," a Northwest Europebased trader said.
Meanwhile, a second Mediterranean-focused trader noted a similar physical fundamental within the West Mediterranean region.
In the ARA region, however, market participants described "logistical constraints" amid limited sales of VLSFO from certain terminals, leading to delays.
In the Northwest European high sulfur fuel oil market, participants described demand as quiet, though no concerns were raised regarding supply-side availability.
In the retail bunker market, appetite for product was described as muted, driven by volatility in Brent futures and fluctuating ZRE-A ticket prices in Rotterdam.
In the Mediterranean high sulfur fuel oil market, opinions on supply were mixed. A third trader described the market as thin in terms of availability, citing "Med tightness" and "no barrels around," and expected these tight conditions to continue throughout the month of October.
A fourth trader took the opposite view, saying, "I think it is quite balanced... I don't see tightness."
There were no trades in the Platts Market on Close assessment process for fuel oil cargoes.

Naphtha prices climb on higher swaps
Backwardation widens most in Nov/Dec spread
The European naphtha complex moved up Oct. 7 tracking higher swaps, with the front-month crack improving for a third consecutive session.
Backwardation widened across the curve, with the Nov/Dec spread seeing the sharpest rise, pointing to particularly acute tightness further along the prompt structure rather than solely at the very front. The broader widening across the curve reinforced signs of limited nearby availability, even as the demand backdrop remained mixed.
East-West dynamics were little changed on the day, with the spread broadly stable across the front months, though slight widening further along the curve pointed to incremental support from Asian demand.
Economics between propane and naphtha as cracker feedstocks continued to shift. The Northwest European propane-naphtha spread narrowed to a six-month high this week on Oct.7, supported by seasonal propane demand, challenging arbitrage economics and elevated freight, which have combined to firm propane values while naphtha has struggled to find similar support. Despite the move, the spread remains well within the range favoring propane over naphtha for flexible crackers.
Naphtha demand from petrochemical players remains subdued, with high flat prices and weak downstream margins continuing to limit cracker buying interest. Gasoline blending demand has also eased as the market moves further from peak summer specifications, removing another source of underlying support.
The octane market remains firm, with MTBE values still elevated amid limited imports, reduced European output and ongoing barge constraints across Northwest Europe.
A jump in flat prices and a third straight day of crack gains contrasted with still-subdued downstream demand, as widening backwardation continues to underpin the European naphtha market, while a narrowing propane-naphtha spread, soft petrochemical demand and quiet gasoline blending interest continue to shape feedstock dynamics heading into the winter season.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the physical naphtha CIF NWE cargo price at $883/mt, up $27.25/mt on the day.
Platts assessed the front-month CIF NWE naphtha crack at minus $3.80/b, up 50 cents/b day over day, with the equivalent swap up $26.75/mt at $841/mt.
Platts assessed the October/November time spread at $34/ mt, up $1.50/mt on the day, while the November/December spread was up $3/mt at $30.50/mt.
Platts is part of S&P Global Energy. -- Harry Deighton

Gas Liquids
NWE propane-naphtha spread narrows to six-month high, propane favored for crackers
Propane-naphtha gap tightens 41.7% in month
Seasonal propane demand lifts values
Naphtha demand capped by weak petchem, softer gasoline blending
European propane's discount to naphtha has narrowed sharply over the past month, as seasonal propane demand strengthened while weaker petrochemical and gasoline blending demand weighed on naphtha fundamentals.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the Northwest European propane-naphtha spread at minus $110.25/mt on Oct. 7, narrowing 41.7% from minus $189/mt on Sept. 7. The spread is at its narrowest level since March 25, but remains well below the minus $60/mt level at which flexible steam crackers typically favor propane over naphtha.
The spread had been deeply negative for much of the year, driven by elevated naphtha flat prices, Middle East supply disruption and previously strong gasoline blending demand, which kept naphtha tight and reinforced propane's economic advantage. The recent move marks a partial reversal from those extreme levels, albeit not enough to shift cracker economics back toward favoring naphtha.
The narrowing has been driven largely by firmer propane fundamentals heading into winter. Seasonal heating demand has supported LPG values, with one trading source describing the European propane market as "strong again." Market participants also pointed to stronger buying interest from petrochemical players as crackers continue to maximize propane intake where possible.
Challenging arbitrage economics have further strained incremental propane supply into Europe. At the same time, higher delivered naphtha costs have further weakened naphtha's appeal to steam crackers, reinforcing the demand-side pressure on naphtha.
Naphtha demand from petrochemical buyers remains subdued, with high flat prices and weak downstream margins limiting cracker appetite. Gasoline blending demand has also eased as the market moves further away from peak summer specifications, removing another source of support for the European naphtha complex.
Despite the recent narrowing, propane remains the more economical feedstock for flexible European steam crackers. The move is also broadly seasonal, with the propane-naphtha spread typically tightening into autumn as heating demand rises. On Oct. 7 last year, the spread was slightly narrower at around minus $102.75/mt, suggesting the current shift is in line with seasonal patterns, even as propane continues to hold a clear economic advantage over naphtha.
-- Harry Deighton, Baran Serdaroglu

US DATA: Propane, propylene stocks drop to lowest since late August as exports rise again
Inventories draw 1.801 million barrels
Exports over 2.4 million b/d
Production falls to 2.904 million b/d
US propane and propylene inventories fell below 108 million barrels for the second time in three weeks during the week ended Oct. 2, as exports surged and overall demand exceeded analyst projections, according to US Energy Information Administration supply and demand data published Oct. 7.
Stocks tightened by 1.801 million barrels to 107.846 million barrels, their lowest level since late August, when stocks hovered at 107.4111 million barrels. This decline follows the previous week's build, in which propane and propylene inventories rose by more than 1.7 million barrels.
The US Gulf Coast accounted for nearly the entire stock draw, with regional stocks down 1.753 million barrels to 68.671 million barrels.
Total demand, including exports and domestic product supplied, rose 483,000 barrels/day to 3.237 million b/d, according to EIA data. Of that total, exports rose 795,000 b/d to 2.464 million b/d, eclipsing 2.4 million b/d for the second time in the last three weeks. Meanwhile, domestic product supplied dropped 312,000 b/d to 773,000 b/d.
The stock draw was directionally opposite to projections by S&P Global Energy CERA analysts, who projected a 532,000-thousand-barrel stock build based on an expected 100,000-b/d rise in demand.
"The stockbuilding would continue to rise seasonally, but the trend is slowing down in fall," CERA analysts said in their Oct. 5 propane market forecast.
The US Gulf Coast stock draw came as US LPG exports rebounded by approximately 41% week over week to 1.8 million mt in the week ended Oct. 2, as Panama Canal constraints eased and vessel availability improved, according to S&P Global Commodities at Sea shipping data. Terminals like Nederland saw volumes more than double to 430,000 mt, while Enterprise Hydrocarbons increased by 35% to 456,000 mt, according to an Oct. 6 CERA analyst report.
Meanwhile, production volumes fell slightly to 2.904 million b/d, though production has yet to fall below 2.9 million b/d since mid-August.
Amid the inventory drawdown, Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, heard of US Gulf Coast propane at Mont Belvieu's Enterprise terminal up 2 cents/gallon to 91.25 cents/gal during morning trading Oct. 7.
-- Hunter Marrow

Platts European Propane Daily Commentary
Europe propane supply tightens in October
Naphtha spread narrows, affects cracker choice
CIF cargo prices jump $35.25/mt daily
Northwest European propane supply remained tight Oct. 7, with outstanding buying requirements keeping attention on cargo availability and the potential for demand to extend into November.
"Europe is tight," a market source said, adding that the supply constraints were "certainly not at the best time."
Buying interest for second-half October deliveries continued to support sentiment, while some petrochemical buyers were understood to have requirements still to cover.
Uncertainty over the extent of outstanding demand kept attention on further purchasing, with early-November requirements also relevant to the supply outlook.
Propane's relationship with naphtha remained central to petrochemical buying decisions.
The narrowing discount reduced propane's advantage for flexible crackers, increasing the importance of relative feedstock

costs when planning purchases. However, the source indicated that the spread for November
onward remained low "vs history," suggesting propane retained a more favorable position further along the curve.
The distinction between prompt and forward economics left buyers weighing immediate procurement needs against the incentive to secure propane for later consumption.
On supply, the closed US-Europe arbitrage constrained additional US arrivals, leaving Europe reliant on supply from North Sea refineries to meet regional requirements.
"Nothing unusual, I would say," a source said when asked about supply flows.
With limited scope for replacement imports, the availability of North Sea volumes remained central to covering outstanding demand and easing regional tightness.
In the CIF propane cargo market, one bid was observed. Gunvor SA bid on a TOT23 cargo, CIF Flushing, for Oct. 20-24 delivery, at a 50% $816/metric ton flat price and a 50% CIF Amsterdam-Rotterdam-Antwerp balance-month next-day plus $45/mt. The bid was deemed competitive and set the assessment.
Platts assessed the CIF large cargo market in outright terms at $812.50/mt, up $35.25/mt day over day, at a $37.50/mt premium to the propane CIF October swap, a $9.25/mt increase in premium levels.
In the propane coaster market, no bids, offers, or trades were heard in the Platts Market on Close assessment process. In the absence of competitive indications, the market was assessed on trader sentiment.
Platts assessed the propane coaster market at $848/mt, up $36/mt day over day, at a $35.50/mt premium to large cargoes.
Platts assessed the FOB ARA barges up 75 cents/mt day over day at plus $35.50/mt versus the CIF large propane market, while FCA ARA market premiums to CIF large were assessed at plus $48.50/mt.
Platts is part of S&P Global Energy. -- Baran Serdaroglu
Platts European Butane Daily Commentary
Tight supply drives butane prices higher
Winter blending demand absorbs spot volumes
US arbitrage remains closed to Europe
Northwest European butane continued to find support on Oct. 7 amid tight supply, building winter blending demand and a lack of spot availability kept the market firmly in sellers' favor.
Buyers struggled to source prompt product, and participants pointed to a fundamental picture that shows little sign of loosening in the near term.
Availability remains the main driver of strength. North Sea supply into the merchant market has been limited and refinery butane output across the region was thin, with several refiners keeping product in-house for their own gasoline pools rather than

offering it into the spot market. On the demand side, the seasonal switch to winter-grade
gasoline was lifting butane consumption. Higher permitted vapor pressure under winter specifications lets blenders take in more butane, which remains one of the cheapest ways to add volume and lift RVP.
Healthy gasoline margins support those blending economics, and blenders were heard actively seeking prompt coasters for late October and November delivery. Blending demand is now absorbing tons that would otherwise have gone to the petrochemical sector, where butane has become less competitive against naphtha as a cracker feedstock.
The transatlantic arbitrage continues to offer little relief. Firm US Gulf Coast butane values and elevated freight costs keep US product largely uneconomic for European buyers. Strong Asian demand continues to pull US cargoes east, and the few US cargoes heading to Europe are mostly propane-heavy loadings that do little to address butane tightness specifically.
Participants expect the market to stay tight into November as winter blending demand builds. Any meaningful easing would likely require the US arbitrage to reopen or North Sea supply to recover. Until then, sentiment remains firm, and blenders rather than crackers continue to drive price direction.
In the butane CIF NWE large cargo market, one bid was heard. Stasco was seen bidding a 12,000-metric ton cargo meeting Lyondell's specifications, for Oct. 28-Nov. 1 delivery, at 102% Amsterdam-Rotterdam-Antwerp balance-month October naphtha. It was deemed competitive and set the assessment.

Platts Western Mediterranean LPG Daily Commentary
West Med butane prices jump $35/mt on supply
Refinery retention limits merchant availability
Winter gasoline blending drives butane demand
West Mediterranean butane sentiment strengthened Oct. 7 as limited spot availability and sustained seasonal demand kept pressure on buyers seeking prompt supply.
"The market is moving up again today," a market source said. Winter-grade gasoline production continued to underpin

butane consumption, while refinery retention restricted the volumes available to external buyers. As refiners directed more material toward their own blending requirements, demand competed for a limited pool of merchant supply.
The shortage of readily available tonnes also constrained trading opportunities. Relatively muted activity reflected the difficulty of sourcing suitable cargoes rather than a marked deterioration in buying appetite, leaving the market sensitive to fresh offers and outstanding requirements.
Higher October contract prices in Algeria added to the pressure on regional procurement costs. The increase raised the cost of securing replacement barrels, reinforcing the importance of local availability for buyers seeking to cover nearby needs.
Demand from other Mediterranean destinations added another consideration for cargo allocation, limiting the scope for available material to relieve tightness across the region. Buyers, therefore, remained focused on the availability of tonnes.
The combination of refinery retention, seasonal blending demand and higher replacement costs maintained a supportive backdrop, with little indication that quieter trading had translated into easier supply.

Russian tanker operators play bigger role
Mainstream companies lack incentives to stay in Russia
Russia increased crude exports in September by relying more heavily on non-G7 tankers, particularly those operated by domestic companies, whose shipments reached their highest

October 7, 2026

level since Western nations imposed a price cap. Tankers flagged, owned and operated by companies not
based in G7 countries and their allies, and not insured by Western protection and indemnity clubs, lifted nearly 2.89 million barrels/ day in September, compared with 2.7 million b/d in August, according to S&P Global Commodities at Sea and Maritime Intelligence Risk Suite data.
The increase came as overall Russian seaborne exports rose to 4.09 million b/d from 3.82 million b/d in the same period.
While the overall share of Russian crude exports on non-G7 tankers, mainly from the shadow flee established to bypass Western sanctions, showed little change month on month, Russian tanker operators were shouldering more responsibilities for their home country's overseas sales.
Continuing the upward trajectory seen in recent months, shipments rose from 18.6 million barrels in August to 20.6 million barrels in September -- the highest since the G7 and EU began banning maritime service firms from participating in Russian crude exports unless the barrels were sold below a certain threshold in December 2022.
State carrier Sovcomflot's tankers loaded 6.4 million barrels in September, while the remainder were generally lifted by tankers owned by small, little-known companies, according to the CAS and MIRS data. All of the ships were flagged in Russia.

Meanwhile, 107 ships were reflagged in Russia between January 2025 and June 2026, 56% of which joined the Russian registry after previously reporting false flag information following designation by Western authorities, according to a study by the Centre for Research on Energy and Clean Air.
"`Shadow' fleet vessels have been funneled into the Russian ship registry as their other options have been slowly closed off," the nonprofit said in a note published in September. "Under diplomatic pressure from sanctioning countries, many open registries began significantly withdrawing their flagging services for `shadow' vessels. With vessels increasingly being removed from open registries, many vessels turned to false flags."

Flag clampdown The International Maritime Organization has launched a
campaign against falsified flag registration in recent quarters. In 2026, some members states -- including Cameroon and the Cook Islands -- told the UN agency that more than 110 ships with a total carrying capacity of 9.78 million deadweight tons were removed from their flag registries or wrongly claimed to be flagged with them.
More than 100 of these were tankers designated by Western authorities for sanctioned Russian trades or other reasons, according to an earlier Platts review of their circulars. Platts is part of S&P Global Energy.

Sanctions impact As for the G7 fleet, Greek-operated tankers loaded 17.3 million
barrels in September, up from 13.6 million barrels in August, according to the data. The increase came as fewer tanker attacks were reported in the Black Sea.
But freight market conditions point to limited financial incentives for mainstream companies to remain in Russian trade going forward. The time-charter-equivalent earnings for Aframaxes shipping Russian oil from the Baltic to West Coast India have been below the Global Aframax Index since Sept. 9, according to Platts assessments.
Moreover, US President Donald Trump on Sept. 18 signed legislation allowing him to impose tariffs of up to 100% on goods imported from the five largest importers of Russian crude or natural gas by volume, and to impose sanctions on tankers used to evade sanctions.
"The measures could potentially increase the number of sanctioned vessels and raise compliance risks for companies involved in transporting Russian crude," shipbroker BRS said in a note in September. "For the oil market, the measure could further disrupt established Russian trade flows, particularly if tighter restrictions on vessels are combined with pressure on major buyers to reduce purchases of Russian crude oil and refined products."

Russia says fire out on tanker hit in Black Sea attack
Tanker fire extinguished, no oil spill
Two civilian ships struck in Bulgarian waters Oct. 6
A Russian Aframax tanker was set ablaze in Russia's Black Sea waters, amid a series of recent attacks, Russia's Ministry of Transport said Oct. 7.
Transport Minister Andrei Nikitin chaired an emergency response meeting following the attack on the tanker Aframax Rio off the Black Sea coast, a ministry announcement said on Telegram.
"Open burning on the vessel has been extinguished. Rescue vessels remain on standby. There is no oil cargo pollution of the surrounding waters," the announcement said. A joint task force comprising personnel from the Ministry of Transport, the Ministry of Emergency Situations, the Ministry of Defence and the Krasnodar region has begun clean-up and damage-limitation operations, the ministry said.
The vessel's destination country is India and it is carrying Urals sour crude, according to ship tracking software S&P Global Commodities at Sea.
Aframax vessels are the workhorse of crude and refined product exports from the region, carrying cargoes of around 80,000-100,000 mt from Russian Black Sea ports, including Novorissiysk -- the primary export terminal for Urals crude -- as well as from the CPC terminal north of Novorissiysk, which handles Kazakh-origin crude.
Disruptions to tanker operations in the area have at times tightened supply flows of both Russian Urals and CPC Blend crude to Mediterranean buyers.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the rate to carry a 135,000 metric ton cargo of crude from the Black Sea to the Mediterranean at $121.69/mt Oct. 6, up 632% year over year and above a five-year average of $17.90/mt.
Platts assessed the crude oil Additional War Risk Premium, for carriage on Aframaxes and Suezmaxes in the Black Sea, at $3/b Oct. 6, up 329% year over year. It has averaged $1.31/b since it was launched in October 2022.
Bulgarian waters In a separate incident, two civilian vessels were attacked
in Bulgarian waters, with one sinking and the other sustaining significant damage, Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy said on X Oct. 6. Zelenskyy said Ukraine's Defence Forces conducted no operations in the area and attributed the attack to Russia, saying it was "a combined Russian attack using maritime and aerial drones."
One of the vessels struck was identified as MV Royad Mammadov in an Oct. 6 statement from India's Ministry of External Affairs. The ministry confirmed that two foreign seafarers were killed in the attack and that crew members, including Indian nationals, were injured.

Strait of Hormuz ship traffic falls Oct. 6 after tanker attacks
Hormuz transits drop to 19 amid attacks Fire damages Panama-flagged tanker Five vessels struck between Oct. 3-6
Ship traffic through the Strait of Hormuz dropped to 19 transits on Oct. 6, a second consecutive decline, down from 23 as ship attacks continue in the narrow waterway, according to the latest report from S&P Global Commodities at Sea.
A fire broke out onboard the Panama-flagged On Peace crude oil tanker as it was struck in the strait Oct. 6, nine nautical miles off the coast of Oman, Vanguard Tech reported. India's Ministry of External Affairs said in a statement that 12 of the 19 crew members were injured in the attack. On Oct. 7, it was anchored off the coast of Fujairah in the UAE, according to CAS data.
"The targeting of commercial shipping, seafarers and civilian infrastructure in the region must end, and navigation and commerce through international waterways must be restored at the earliest," the statement said.
Of the vessels that sailed through the strait on Oct. 6, nine vessels entered the waterway and 10 exited the Strait of Hormuz.
One LNG tanker exited the strait Oct. 6 for the first time in two days; it loaded at Qatar's Ras Laffan on Oct. 4, transited the Iran-approved route with its AIS on and is currently headed to Pakistan, the report said. So far in October, four LNG tankers have left the Strait of Hormuz.
Five tankers were struck in the Strait of Hormuz over Oct. 3-6, with one vessel turning around after it was told to do so by the navy of the Islamic Revolution Guard Corps, according to multiple notices from UK Maritime Trade Organization (UKMTO) and Vanguard Tech.
Just beyond the Strait of Hormuz at the US blockade line, 58 vessels crossed the line, with 31 of those entering the Gulf and moving toward the Strait of Hormuz, the report said.
"Hydrocarbon-related movements included 19 inbound crude, LNG and LPG tankers and 12 outbound tankers," the report said. Only one of those tankers was identified as linked to Iran.

-- Lauren Holtmeier
Vitol CEO warns of shipping crisis as Middle East oil flows hit 14 million b/d
Ship-to-ship transfers create inefficiencies
Freight costs hit records amid tight supply
Oil flows through the Middle East have reached as much as 14 million barrels/day over the past seven to 10 days, but the way in which transits through the Strait of Hormuz are conducted can intensify shipping bottlenecks and further increase freight costs, Vitol CEO Russell Hardy said Oct.6.
With more tankers from the private sector and Gulf states' national fleets willing to take transit risks in the contested waters, Hardy said 2 million b/d of refined products and 12 million b/d of crude were exported from the Middle East in the period.
"Obviously, it's still a very difficult and dangerous situation. And that's why the market remains elevated because there's still no obvious solution in front of us," Hardy said, addressing the Energy Intelligence Forum in London.
His comments came as Iran launched another wave of ship attacks near Hormuz, the chokepoint handling 20% of global seaborne oil flows in peace times. The UK Maritime Trade Operations recorded 15 security incidents between Sept. 29 and Oct. 5.
"The world is still short of refining capacity, principally because of the hits on Russian infrastructure, and the loss of five months of normal refining runs in the Middle East," the CEO said, adding that refining runs have started to gradually return.
"So, there is perhaps 2 million barrels of day of products coming out of the Middle East region, which is helping to rebalance products a little bit. But products are still in a very, very tight situation, and that will probably roll forward [a] little bit longer as we go through winter," Hardy said.
Despite the recovery, Hardy said Middle Eastern oil product exports increasingly rely on offshore ship-to-ship transfers before delivery to end-customers.
"So, it's just a very inefficient process, and there's really not quite enough shipping to go around," he said.
Middle Eastern crude exports via STS transfer in the Gulf of Oman reached 7.15 million b/d over Sept. 1-28, up from 4.17 million b/d in August, according to S&P Global Commodities at Sea data.
The Middle East has "re-engineered" the way it is conducting business and shuttling oil out "as a matter of national priority and customer priority," Hardy said.
However, despite the increase in Hormuz transits, the danger of these shipping activities has not significantly reduced, according to the CEO.
"So, we had sort of pretty parabolic pricing on shipping, which is creating a lot of stress for everybody because nobody really knows [...] how much their shipping costs are going to be, which

creates a difficult set of decisions for people to make day-to-day." The VLCC rate for transporting 270,000 metric tons of crude
from the Gulf to China reached $251.56/metric ton Oct. 6, while that for shipping 265,000 mt to west coast India amounted to $96.56/mt, according to data from Platts, part of S&P Global Energy. Both prices were at their record highs, Platts data showed.
The Middle Eastern oil flows are "an important lifeline," considering how low the world's inventories are, Hardy said.
"Without it, you do have that $200 a barrel scenario. It's pretty important that it continues. Many national oil companies have committed to maintaining exports," he added.

LR1s jump higher

East of Suez Long Range tankers were higher Oct. 7 as more shipowners are exploring the possibility of turning some of their LR2s dirty in the perennial quest for higher returns, market participants said. This would imply lesser availability of tankers to move clean cargoes, they said.
A dirty fixture was cited in the market wherein an Aframax was placed on subjects at $14 million for the Nov. 5 loading on the Vancouver-China route. It creates new possibilities altogether when it comes to switching from the clean segment to the dirty segment. "The market is super firm," a clean oil tankers' broker said.
Bab al-Mandab and the Strait of Hormuz should therefore attract further premium, said an executive with a global commodities trading company. For a westbound cargo to be loaded in Oman, an offer was heard at around $16 million, the executive said. Refined products cargoes for loading in Oman are competing with their counterparts in clean and dirty segments in other regions, where, despite much lower geopolitical risks, the rates are astronomical, the executive said.
In a recent fixture, the Torm Emma was placed on subjects at w550 by a Bapco-Total joint venture, BXT, for the Oct. 20 naphtha loading on the STS Sohar-Japan route, sources said. Loadings inside the Persian Gulf commanded a significant premium over the Gulf of Oman, but no fixtures were heard. LR1s have been untested inside the Persian Gulf for more than two weeks now, as market participants are cautious amid recent attacks around the Strait of Hormuz. Nevertheless, a section of the shipping industry believed that this should make transportation across the risky waters even more costly. Owners willing to move their ships through the Strait of Hormuz are seeking higher freight due to heightened risk perception, while charterers insist that lower demand should result in a downward correction.
"Owners are expected to dig in their heels and keep the [LR] freight higher for as long as possible," one of the brokers said in a daily report.

VLCC rates firm on Oct loading demand
Charterers split stems amid tight tonnage
Charterers downsize Aframax cargoes to MR vessels
The East of Suez very large crude carrier market continued to show a firmer tone on Oct. 7, supported by healthy loading demand for the last 10 days of October.
Among the overnight fixtures, ST was heard to have taken the DHT Antelope on subjects for an Oct. 22 loading from the Fujairah ship-to-ship transfer area or the West Coast India ship-to-ship transfer area to Onsan at w825.
Many VLCC cargoes were believed to be active outside the visible market, sources said.
Owners said charterers' main priority was securing tonnage, with freight costs becoming a secondary consideration.
The trickle-down effect across vessel classes was becoming increasingly evident.
With VLCC availability tightening and rates remaining at higher levels, some charterers were forced to split VLCC stems into Suezmax cargoes.
The Suezmax market was unchanged Oct. 7, while sentiment remained strong. "Suezmaxes remain firm, but assessing exact market rates is difficult," a Suezmax broker said. Although no fresh cargoes were heard, some VLCC stems were seeking to split into Suezmax cargoes, supporting the sector's fundamentals, sources said.
Among fresh fixtures, the Poliegos and the Captain Lyristis were heard to have been taken by Exxon at w900 for Oct. 22 and Oct. 25 loadings, respectively, on the Oman-East route.
Aframaxes were mostly firm and assessed higher in the Southeast Asian and Australian loading markets, as numerous cargoes remained uncovered and no fresh fixtures were heard. "Across Southeast Asia, high freight levels are leading to charterers attempting to downsize Aframax cargoes into medium range vessels," an Aframax broker said. Southeast Asian loadings were likely to rise further, given that short-haul voyages were achieving higher rates, an owner said, citing an Aframax placed on subjects by BCPT at $3.5 million for an Oct. 17 loading from the Linggi ship-to-ship transfer area to Thailand. Higher rates were also heard for west-to-east loadings, the same owner added.
In another fixture, an Aframax was heard to have been

taken by ST for an Oct. 27 loading from Seria to Singapore, with discharge options in Malaysia and Thailand. The rate was not heard.
Among fresh cargoes, Petrolimex was heard seeking an Aframax for an Oct. 21 loading from Singapore to South Korea, sources said.
Persian Gulf Aframaxes remained firm day over day. "There aren't many owners working cargoes within the Persian Gulf, and the risk of heading inside to load cargoes outweighs the benefits of doing so," an Aframax broker said, adding that voyages outside the Gulf were also commanding high rates.
Among fixtures heard overnight, the Swan Lake was heard to have been taken by Vitol at w710 for an Oct. 27 loading from Bashayer to destinations in the East. However, an Aframax broker said the fixture might not be repeatable, adding that the market was likely to command higher levels for vessels loading in the early-November window.
Medium Range freight levels were higher, supported by firm Aframaxes. Multiple brokers said charterers were splitting Aframax cargoes into MR parcels, supporting the sector.

Tanker rates plummet amid weak demand
USGC-UKC route drops w60 to w300 level
Rates for the Americas Medium Range clean tankers saw the largest declines of the week on Oct. 7 amid persistently weak shipping demand, which maintained a predominantly bearish sentiment, although market participants pointed to the potential for a modest improvement in demand.
As in previous days, sources continued to indicate that rates would fall below last-done levels, with no immediate sign of stability if limited activity allowed containers to accumulate on the US Gulf Coast.
According to shipbroker position lists, there were seven tankers available for prompt loading, along with an average of 20 additional units in the standard three-to-10-day loading window.
"I haven't seen anything done, but it seems softer," a shipbroker said. "With three days of limited inquiry, the list is getting longer."
The declines seen during the day followed a recent deal done Oct. 6, in which Sunoco placed the Energy Afrodite on subjects for an Oct. 10-12 USGC-Transatlantic run at w330.
Market indications heard after the deal initially considered the w330 mark reflective of the market. However, as the day progressed, lower levels became increasingly expected, alongside market talk of lower rates being achieved. By the end of the Oct. 7 Platts Market on Close assessment process, market calls for the transatlantic runs were heard at w300.
Platts assessed rates for the benchmark 38,000 mt USGC-UK Continent run at w300, down w60 day over day, narrowing the rate spread with the related USGC-Brazil run to w70, with rates for the latter assessed at w370.

Platts assessed freight for the benchmark USGC-Chile voyage at $3.6 million, down $200,000 from Oct. 6.
Although tonnage availability remained high, market sources indicated that a substantial volume of products still needed to be moved, creating the possibility of near-term demand growth.
"The list is long, but I think there is still a decent amount of volume that needs covering," a second shipbroker said.

Platts Americas Dirty Tanker Daily Commentary
VLCC USGC-China run surges to record $81 million Suezmax Guyana-UKC route falls w50 amid testing Aframax Vancouver-China run hits $14 million

Freight for the Americas VLCC segment reached new record highs on Oct. 7 as tight tonnage availability met increased demand for oil heading East, allowing owners to secure significantly higher rates.
The latest deal reported saw Cosmo Oil book the Esteem Explorer for a Nov. 19-21 loading US Gulf Coast-East run at $81 million, with demurrage at $810,000/day. Prior to that, SK Energy booked the Sea Jade for Nov. 2-7 loading dates at $77 million, and Trafigura booked the Alexandros for Nov. 19-21 dates at $76 million.
"No one is taking VLCCs at these prices on speculation ... all the deals have confirmed buyers paying the freight/oil costs," one shipbroker said.
Regarding the sky-high rates, a second broker said, "it's like the wild wild west out here."
Following the last-done deal, market indications largely hovered around $81 million, with a second shipbroker anticipating next-done levels reaching $84 million-$85 million.
"We may see a $100 million cargo," a third shipbroker said. A fourth shipbroker said that the rise would have to stop at some point, "otherwise it doesn't make sense to produce oil." Platts assessed the benchmark 270,000 metric ton USGCChina run at a record high $81 million, rising $16 million on the day and 62% on the week. The fourth broker said charterers looking to book VLCCs were left with few alternatives, as "severe shortages of Suezmax and Aframax tonnage have substantially reduced the effectiveness of traditional upsizing and downsizing strategies." Rates for the Suezmaxes saw mixed movements on the day. Despite a reportedly still tight position list in the Atlantic Basin, overall muted activity left some participants questioning if rates for transatlantic voyages had reached a ceiling. The slightly bearish sentiment was confirmed by reports that Shell booked the Aegean Dream for an Oct. 24-26 loading Guyana-UK Continent/Mediterranean run at w950. Platts assessed the benchmark 130,000 mt Guyana-UKC run down w50 at w950. However, calls for the Suezmax voyages heading East continued to climb alongside strength in the VLCC segment. Freight for the 130,000 mt USGC-Singapore run finished the day up $1 million to $37 million on firmer indications.

Four long-haul fixtures were reported out of Vancouver; the latest came from ExxonMobil, booked on a to-be-nominated Vitol ship to load Nov. 6 for a Vancouver-Far East run at $14 million.
Participants unanimously agreed the deal was the last done. Platts assessed the benchmark 80,000 mt Vancouver-China route at $14 million, marking the fourth consecutive record high for the benchmark, having risen from $8.25 million on Oct. 2.
Market indications for the Vancouver-US West Coast route also rose despite limited fresh testing, as higher achieved levels to the Far East and strong rates in adjacent markets supported rates, sources said. Platts assessed the benchmark 80,000 mt Vancouver-USWC run at w800, also a record high.
"The vessels that are there ... will not be offering discounts to anyone," a fifth shipbroker said. "Getting last done is not going to be easy."
Rates in the Atlantic Basin were steady, with Platts assessing the benchmark 70,000 USGC-UKC route unchanged at w1,000 based on the majority of indications heard at that level.
Six Aframax fixtures destined to the East were reported on Oct. 7. Suncor also booked the Siena for an Argentina-East run at w850 and Vitol booked the Riverside for a USGC-East run at $27 million.

Kazakhstan aims to end refined product imports, start exports by 2030
Kazakhstan wants to end fuel imports by 2030
Refineries cover more than 90% of needs
Major refinery expansions planned
Kazakhstan plans to eliminate its dependence on imported refined products and start exports by 2030, by expanding domestic refining capacity, Energy Minister Yerlan Akkenzhenov said Oct. 7.
In 2025, the country's refineries processed 18.4 million metric tons of crude and produced about 15.5 million mt of finished petroleum products, the minister said during a livestreamed address to the Kurultai, Kazakhstan's unicameral parliament. This accounts for more than 90% of Kazakhstan's domestic fuel needs, according to Akkenzhenov.
"We face annual challenges regarding the supply of aviation fuel. At present, we import around 300,000 tons of aviation fuel from the Russian Federation, and the [renewed] agreement enables us to import aviation fuel from Russia duty-free," Akkenzhenov said.
Kazakhstan's parliament ratified Oct. 7 the renewal of the intergovernmental agreement with Russia on the supply of oil and petroleum products from the country. The deal was extended until Jan. 1, 2028, with automatic renewal for five-year periods in the absence of objections from either party.
The agreement provides for the continued annual coordination of indicative balances for the supply of oil and petroleum products, as well as for the duty-free import of petroleum products from Russia to Kazakhstan.
However, the minister hopes Kazakhstan will be able to meet all of its needs in less than four years.
There are currently three large oil refineries in Kazakhstan and around 25 mini-refineries, according to the minister. "But practically none of [mini-refineries] produce fuel that meets the quality standards established by the Customs Union's technical regulations," he added.
The large refineries include the 6 million mt/y Pavlodar, the 6 million mt/y Shymkent and the 5.5 million mt/year Atyrau.
"We plan to increase the capacity of the Shymkent refinery from 6 million to 12 million tons. We expect the feasibility study

for this refinery to be completed by the end of this year, at which point we anticipate moving to the practical implementation stage," Akkenzhenov said.
"We also plan to expand the Pavlodar refinery by 2 million tons and are addressing bottlenecks at the Atyrau refinery-- specifically, the construction of additional diesel hydrotreating lines," the minister added, stressing that these additional volumes should enable Kazakhstan to fully meet its domestic refining needs.
By 2030, Kazakhstan is expected not only to meet its own domestic needs but also to export petroleum products to Central Asian countries, according to the minister.
The deadline and volume targets differ slightly from figures presented by Chairman of the Board of KazMunayGas Askhat Khasenov at a meeting with Prime Minister Olzhas Bektenov Sept. 23. The Pavlodar refinery should be expanded by 3 million mt/y, with completion expected by 2031, Khasenov said at the meeting, according to the official website of the prime minister.
KazMunayGas was not immediately available for comment. The Atyrau refinery will be expanded by 600,000 mt to 6.1 million mt/year, according to the KazMunayGas chairman. Expansion projects at three refineries will increase their combined capacity from 17.5 million mt to 28.8 million mt, according to the statement from KazMunayGas. The 2030 export goal does not include the fourth refinery which is also under consideration, with three areas chosen as a potential future home for the facility. "We are considering several sites within the Republic of Kazakhstan: in the Ulytau, Atyrau, and Mangystau regions. Consultants are currently working on this," Akkenzhenov said. Kazakhstan's government hopes to attract private investment for its new refinery in exchange for guarantees including tax incentives and feedstock supplies, the ministry's director of the oil transportation and refining department, Daulet Arykbayev, told Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, Sept. 30. If approved, the 10 million mt/y fourth large refinery is expected to start up by 2033. The ministry plans a "major roadshow" for the project in 2027-2028, according to Arykbayev.
-- Alexandra Vladimirova

Japan cuts fuel subsidies by 16% for Oct. 8-14 amid weaker crude
Subsidy decreases ¥8.30/liter to ¥42.40/liter
Gasoline pump prices unchanged at ¥170.20/liter
Japan cut its fuel subsidy for oil products to ¥42.40/liter (27 cents/liter) for the week of Oct. 8-14, from ¥50.70/liter the previous week, amid weaker crude oil prices, the government said Oct. 7.
The government calculates the weekly subsidy using data that includes the average retail gasoline price, as surveyed every Monday, crude oil prices and other factors. It provides a full subsidy for the portion exceeding ¥170/liter ($1.07/liter).
The national average retail price for regular gasoline remained unchanged week over week at ¥170.20/liter as of Oct. 5, according to the Oil Information Center of the Institute of Energy Economics, Japan.
The Ministry of Economy, Trade and Industry used Nikkei Dubai as the benchmark for calculating subsidies from Jan. 27, 2022, to March 18, 2026, and ICE Brent from March 19 to June 3.
METI informed trading houses and wholesalers in a May 27 email, seen by Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, that the subsidy amount would be calculated using Platts Dubai as the benchmark, starting with payments made June 4.
The Platts front-month cash Dubai assessment fell 8% week over week to $107.88/barrel over Sept. 29-Oct. 5.
In March, due to an increase in crude oil prices driven by

the Middle East conflict, the government reinstated gasoline subsidies to maintain the retail price of regular gasoline at ¥170/ liter after ending them on Dec. 30, 2025, according to documents released by METI on March 11.
A fixed subsidy of ¥5/liter was provided for kerosene and fuel oil and was scheduled to end on March 31. However, the government has been providing these fuels with the same subsidy as gasoline since March 19.
For gasoil, the provisional tax rate of ¥17.10/liter was abolished on April 1, according to METI. To prevent demand fluctuations caused by the price drop from the tax abolition, METI said Nov. 7, 2025, that it would provide a subsidy of ¥17.10/liter, equal to the provisional tax rate, from Nov. 27, 2025, to March 31, 2026.
Since March 19, when the gasoline subsidy was reinstated, gasoil received a subsidy of ¥17.10/liter plus the same amount as gasoline. From April 1, the gasoil subsidy was set at the same level as the gasoline subsidy, according to METI.
Japan launched the subsidy program for gasoline, kerosene, gasoil, and fuel oil in January 2022 to mitigate the impact of high oil prices on the economy as it recovered from the pandemic. In April 2022, the program was expanded to include jet fuel and LPG.
Refiners use the subsidy to curb increases in the weekly wholesale prices of oil products, while trading houses deduct it from the selling prices of imported oil products.
The Platts-assessed average prices across the Chiba, Kanagawa, Chukyo, and Hanshin regions on Oct. 7 are as follows: gasoline at ¥99,150/kl ($99.51/barrel), kerosene at ¥118,250/ kl, gasoil at ¥100,800/kl, low-sulfur A-fuel oil at ¥100,775/kl and A-fuel oil at ¥100,275/kl.
-- Hiroyuki Koshoji

OIL FUTURES: Crude slides after IEA offers support for G7 diesel release ...from page 1
East supply concerns mounted following a wave of fresh attacks on shipping in the Strait of Hormuz.
Ship traffic through the Strait of Hormuz fell for a second straight day to 19 transits on Oct. 6, according to the latest report from S&P Global Commodities at Sea.
"There is a clear tug-of-war at the moment between improving supply from the region and lingering threats to supply," ING's Warren Patterson said in a note Oct. 7. "For now, the market is likely to remain nervous to any potential supply disruptions."
Global Risk Management's Arne Lohmann Rasmussen said, "Higher exports in September do not mean that geopolitical risk has declined -- quite the opposite. The narrative may be shifting from rising exports to a growing risk that the current high export levels cannot be sustained."
Oil exports from the Gulf region could return to pre-conflict levels by mid-2027, but a prolonged disruption to shipments could further strain already depleted inventories and lift prices to levels that may threaten demand, the World Bank said in its latest East Asia and Pacific Economic Update Oct. 6.
Erik Meyersson, chief emerging markets strategist at SEB Strategy & Economic Research, wrote in his Conflict Update Oct. 7 that "questions are being raised over the sustainability of the US military operation to secure [Strait of Hormuz] transit, protect allies in the region, and retain geopolitical dominance from an Iranian regime that appears more risktolerant."
Meyersson said this continues to feed into the risk premium and means that "predicting an endgame to the conflict has become more difficult."

US crude stocks hit three-week low US crude oil stocks fell 3.19 million barrels to a three-week
low 424.13 million barrels during the week to Oct. 2, US Energy Information Administration data showed Oct. 7, against a backdrop of stronger exports and refinery demand.
The draw exceeded recent market expectations after the American Petroleum Institute on Oct. 6 reported a 2.09 million barrel decline over the period and put stocks below the seasonal five-year average for the first time since early August.
The draw was predicated on stronger US crude exports, which surged 33% to a four-week high 4.77 million b/d, EIA data showed. Notably, inventories at the NYMEX delivery hub of Cushing, Oklahoma, climbed 440,000 barrels to 24.75 million barrels -- a 20-week high.
-- Christopher Vanmoessner

REFINERY NEWS: Ukraine military reports damage at Astrakhan after strike
Refinery: Astrakhan, near the Caspian Sea, Russia
Owner: Gazprom
Overall capacity: 4 million mt/year (80,000 b/d)
The Ukrainian military said Oct. 6 that, following an Oct. 4 strike, components of two natural gas purification units and of a secondary distillation unit were damaged at the Astrakhan gas condensate processing plant.
Astrakhan has been a target of repeated drone strikes and has been offline since September 2025.
Recently, it resumed sales on the St. Petersburg exchange and was commencing a restart, according to Russia-based market sources with knowledge of the matter.
-- Elza Turner

REFINERY NEWS: Uzbekistan's Fergana targets higher utilization by year-end
Refinery: Fergana, Uzbekistan
Owner: State-controlled
Overall capacity: 1.3 million mt/year (about 26,000 b/d)
Uzbekistan's Fergana refinery targets increasing its utilization from 40% to 65% towards the end of the year and keep raising it further, according to a statement published on the country's presidency site Oct. 6.
According to the statement, Fergana increased its diesel, bitumen and jet fuel output following its recent maintenance, the statement said.
The refinery said that it completed maintenance of key units in late September. During the turnaround, it increased its storage capacity by 45%. The launch of a gasoil hydrotreater allowed it to increase its daily diesel output by 60% to 1,200 mt.
The refinery has been under state control since the second quarter of 2026, according to the refinery's website. It was previously managed by the Saneg company, when it started producing jet fuel A-1 using synthetic kerosene GTL component and environmentally clean summer and winter diesel, according to the refinery, which also noted that it is undergoing modernization.
Fergana has been undergoing large-scale modernization, aiming to increase its capacity to more than 2 million mt/yr and reach 75% light products yield.
-- Elza Turner, Vladislav Vorotnikov
REFINERY NEWS: France's Gonfreville restarts unit
Refinery: Gonfreville l'Orcher, Normandy, France
Owner: TotalEnergies
Overall capacity: 247,000 barrels/day
During a unit restart at France's Gonfreville, noise and steam could occur until late afternoon of Oct. 7, the refinery said late Oct. 6.
It previously announced halting a unit on Sept. 25.

The company had said that Gonfreville is deferring a major maintenance until 2027.
-- Elza Turner
REFINERY NEWS: Vitol restarts Fujairah refinery: Middle East head
Refinery: Fujairah, UAE
Capacity: 100,000 b/d
Owners: Vitol
Vitol's refinery at Fujairah in the UAE is "back up and operating" after a temporary shutdown, Tom Baker, head of Middle East for Vitol, told Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, at a conference in Fujairah Oct. 7.
"It was down for a period, and now it is back up and operating," Baker said at the Energy Markets Forum. He declined to elaborate on the operating rate or how long the refinery was shut.
Fujairah has been attacked during the war in the Middle East but has also attracted new investment to take advantage of its location on the UAE's east coast outside the Strait of Hormuz.
In July, DP World, which operates the Jebel Ali port in Dubai inside the Persian Gulf, announced plans to expand with two new terminals in Fujairah under a 50-year concession with the Fujairah Ports Authority. The project includes a new deep-water trade gateway capable of handling Ultra Large Container Vessels and is expected to take 24 to 30 months once construction starts, DP World said.
-- Elza Turner, Claudia Carpenter

Platts proposes amending freight calculation for Oman, Murban CFR North Asia crude assessments Nov. 2
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, is proposing to amend the freight calculation used in its daily Oman and Murban CFR North Asia crude assessments, effective Nov. 2, 2026.
Under the proposed change, Platts would use the daily VLCC Gulf of Oman-to-Far East freight assessment in $/mt (DOOEW00), which was launched Aug. 3, in its calculation of the outright price of Oman and Murban delivered into North Asia at 4:30 pm Singapore time (0830 GMT).
The proposed change would better reflect the shipping economics for the two grades, with Murban primarily loading from Fujairah port and Oman from Mina Al Fahal port outside the Strait of Hormuz.
Currently, the freight calculation is based on the daily VLCC Persian Gulf-to-Far East freight assessment in $/mt (TDAAB00).
The freight calculation would continue to use the conversion factor for metric tons to barrels, as applied to the grades, in line with their prevailing qualities. The conversion factors are currently 7.32 for Oman and 7.62 for Murban.
In line with this change, Platts is also proposing to use the daily VLCC Gulf of Oman-to-Far East worldscale freight assessment (DIOEW00) in the freight calculation for Murban in its Indonesian Minas, Cinta and Widuri crude market parity prices.
Under the current MPP methodology for Minas, Cinta and Widuri, Murban -- as one of five basket grades -- is normalized for delivery to Singapore, accounting for freight. This is currently calculated using the daily VLCC Persian Gulf-to-Singapore worldscale freight assessment (PFAOD00).
The affected assessments appear in Crude Oil Marketwire, Oilgram Price Report, LNG Daily, Platts OPR World Crude Table, Platts Global Alert/Platts Crude Oil Alert pages 1250, 1805, 2202, 2203, 2211, 2216, 2217, 2244, 2245, 2264, 2265, 2280, 2281, 2811 and 2812, LNG Alert pages 0050 and 0051, Platts Natural Gas Alert pages 1024 and 1069, and under the price database codes below:

Assessments Oman CFR North Asia Murban CFR North Asia Minas Singapore close Minas London close Cinta Singapore close Cinta London close Widuri Singapore close Widuri London close Minas FOB Indonesia $/MMBtu

Daily PCAQJ00 PCAQE00 PCABO00 AAPAZ00 PCAAX00 AAPBJ00 PCAFE00 AAPBN00 LCABO00

Platts to launch daily FOB Middle East bitumen assessments from Nov. 2
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, will launch daily VG30 and VG40 bitumen spot assessments for FOB Middle East, effective Nov. 2, 2026.
The new assessments will complement Platts' existing daily and weekly bitumen assessments, providing greater price transparency and information in the waterborne trade for the product in the Asia-Pacific and Middle East markets.
Platts will launch two new daily outright assessments and their respective monthly averages. These assessments will reflect bulk viscosity grade 30 and VG40 grade bitumen, as per IS 73:2013 specifications, loading 10-30 days forward from the date of publication in the Middle East.

Assessment FOB Middle East Bitumen VG30 FOB Middle East Bitumen VG40

Quantity 2,500-10,000 mt, bulk 2,500-10,000 mt, bulk

Laycan 10-30 days forward 10-30 days forward

UoM $/mt $/mt

Pricing information for material with other specifications, such as PEN 60/70, may be considered for the assessments, normalized to the proposed standard.
The assessments will reflect market value at the close of Asian trading at 4:30 pm Singapore time and follow the Singapore publication schedule. Bids, offers and trades reported throughout the day may be considered in the assessment process, with values normalized to the market close at 4:30 pm Singapore time.
This follows a proposal published Sept. 14 and available here. Please send any questions, comments or feedback to asia_products@spglobal.com, with a copy to pricegroup@spglobal.com. Platts proposes to amend rollover date of Asian Dated Brent Strip at London close
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, is proposing to amend the rollover date of its Asian Dated Brent Strip at London close assessment to the first publication day of the month, from Dec. 1, 2026.
The proposed change, which follows Platts review of historical data sets, would align the ADB Strip at London close assessment with the current rollover date of its ADB Strip at Asia close assessment.
Currently, this assessment, which reflects the forward value of Dated Brent two months ahead of the date of publication at the 4:30 pm London close, rolls over on the 9th of the month.
The assessments affected by this change appear in Crude Oil Marketwire, Asia Pacific/Arab Gulf Marketscan, Oilgram Price Report, Platts Global Alert/ Platts Crude Oil Alert pages 2206, 2213, 2215, 2217, 2219, 2260, 2261, 2262, 2263, 2264, 2265, 2266, 2267, 2268, 2269, 2270, 2271, 2280, 2281, 2558, 2559, and under their price database codes as below:

Units of measurements and descriptions updated for Platts FOLicht ethanol prices The following Platts FOLicht ethanol prices have had their Units of measurements and descriptions updated, as summarized below. Hectoliter Pure Alcohol (HPA) · Type: Volume Unit · Definition: A unit of volume that measures the volume of pure (100%) alcohol. One HLTPA equals 1 hectoliter (100 liters) of pure alcohol. Unlike a standard hectoliter (hL), which measures the total volume of any liquid, HLTPA refers only to the pure alcohol content, regardless of the concentration in the original beverage.

Fossil fuels dominate the energy mix representing over 80% of global energy demand.
